PRELIMINARY REMARKS
It has now been more than a hundred years since L. Feer, in his article
“Les Jatakas” (/.A. 7e Sér., v, 1875), established that a large body of
“extra-canonical” popular Buddhist stories called Pafifidsa Jataka (‘‘Fifty
Birth-stories of the Buddha’’) existed in Southeast Asia, and that this
material probably originated with monks in Chieng Mai (northern Thai-
land) around the fifteenth century, A.D. In 1917 L. Finot published a
concordance of three recensions of these stories, one from the Laotian
and two from the Pali. His work showed that while many of the tales
in the various recensions were similar, each text nevertheless represented
a distinct tradition which must be studied and evaluated on an individual
basis. There are three Pali versions of the Parividsa Jataka (=PJ/), originating
in Cambodia, Thailand and Burma, respectively. The Cambodian and Thai
collections seem to draw upon a common source, as the texts of both are
almost identical; that from Burma, however, differs both in its selection
of stories and in the details of those stories which overlap. Indeed the
Burmese version, popularly known as Zimmé Panndsa (=ZP) (“‘Chieng Mai
Fifty’’) has remained something of a mystery to scholars; only one printed
edition has ever appeared, and that publication, an octavo of 685 pp. put
out by the Hanthawaddy Press, Rangoon, in 1911, was without any
introduction, reference to the manuscript upon which it was based, or
evén the name of the editor. The Rangoon ZP has long been out of print.
According to officials of the press involved, moreover, both the original
manuscript and many of its published copies were lost during the Second
World War; thus only a few such copies are to be found today even in
Burma.
I first became interested in the problem of the PJ upon reading Mme.
G. Terral’s excellent article on the Cambodian and Burmese versions on
one story therefrom, the Samuddaghosajataka (BEFEO, xviii, 1, 1956,
249-351). This article made use of several Cambodian manuscripts, thus
giving what amounted to a critical edition of the Cambodian material; the
Burmese portion, however, was based solely upon the Rangoon edition
referred to above. While in Burma during 1961, therefore, I initiated a
search for further manuscripts of the ZP; results, however, were not
immediately forthcoming. Apparently, if we are to accept an old oral
tradition reported to me by the chief abbot of Pagan, King Mindon of
Mandalay (1853-78) had disapproved of this apocryphal work; thus

vi PRELIMINARY REMARKS
the monasteries had ceased to preserve copies thereof and had also
discontinued its translation into Burmese, with the result that almost no
manuscripts whatever were any longer to be found. Finally, in 1962, a
single manuscript of 324 palmleaves was discovered by the venerable
U Wathawa in the Zetawun monstery in Monywe (in Monywa district)
near Mandalay. The manuscript is complete, and is dated Sakkaraj 1169,
i.e. A.D. 1807. I was able to obtain a photocopy of the entire work through
the courtesy of the Venerable U Wathawa and U Maung Maung Tin of the
University of Mandalay. In spite of many close similarities, this Mandalay
manuscript is not the basis for the Rangoon publication; thus we are now
in a position to produce a critical edition of the ZP, beginning with the
present volume. Eventually, it is to be hoped, a comparative study of all
versions of the PJ will be possible; the Burmese material is of first priority,
however, not only because of the fact that only one manuscript has
heretofore been available, but also because of the greater literary merit
of the Burmese version. The P/ as a whole should prove to have a value
far beyond the sphere of comparative philology, particularly with reference
to the Sanskrit Avadana literature and to various aspects of popular
Southeast Asian Buddhism. (See my article “The story of Sudhana and
Manohara: an analysis of the texts and the Borobudur reliefs,” (BSOAS,
xxix, 3, 1966, 533-558) for a discussion of the connection between one
of these stories and certain popular dramatic performances of Burma and
Thailand.)
Although the Rangoon edition has already appeared in print, I have
nevertheless elected to use this version as the main text, giving variant
readings from the unpublished Mandalay manuscript in the footnotes.
This decision derives mainly from the substantially lower incidence of
inaccuracies (e.g. metrical errors, misspellings, scribal omissions) in the
former, rather than from any distinction in terms of literary quality.
The present volume contains the first twenty five stories of the ZP,
the remaining twenty five will appear in volume II. A critical introduction
to the overall work, dealing with literary sources and linguistic peculiarities
of the language, will be found at the end of the second volume.
September 1978 Padmanabh S. Jaini
; Professor of Buddhist Studies,
University of California, Berkeley.

anonymously and published by the Hanthawaddy
